What does a UI designer do?
As a User Interface designer, you are required to have 
certain skills. A UI/UX Designer's work responsibilities range 
from gathering user needs to creating unique designs. The 
following is a list of UI/UX Designer roles and 
responsibilities:
Gathering and evaluating user requirements, in 
collaboration with product managers and 
engineers.
Illustrating design ideas using storyboards, 
process flows, and sitemaps
Designing graphic user interface elements, like 
menus, tabs, and widgets
Designing UI elements such as input controls, 
navigational components, and informational 
components
Communicate with product and engineering 
teams, as well as business stakeholders and 
executive leadership
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Ensure the voice of the customer is present by 
incorporating customer feedback, usage metrics, 
and usability findings into design to enhance 
user experience
Continuous monitoring and conducting 
numerous audience studies to understand user 
profiles
Being aware of the latest design innovations
Identifying and troubleshooting UX problems 
(e.g., responsiveness)

UX Design
So, let’s start with User Experience Design. What is User 
Experience design?

Well, Wikipedia describes UX design something like this – 
“User Experience design or say – UX, UXD, UED or XD is the 
process of enhancing user satisfaction with a product by 
improving the usability, accessibility, and pleasure provided 
in the interaction with the product.”

Let me break this even more for you guys – User experience 
design is, making something enjoyable and usable between 
the product (Phone, Website, Table, etc.) and the user who’s 
using it. Suppose for example, you’re sitting on a chair and 
somebody had to put a thought into how it would be a 
better experience for you to sit on that chair.

I really feel that the term User Experience design was kind of 
like adopted by the Tech community in the 90s, when Don 
Norman, who is a famous author of one of my favourite 
books called – “The Design Of Everyday Things”, started
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide
referring UX Design as relating to Companies and their 
products and that was during the digital boon.

So this make sense that now a days, when we talk about 
user experience design, it’s mainly spoken about within the 
tech industries (of websites and applications) mostly and not 
on the real world or any other industry. Although it’s been 
adopted by the tech communities, it has a larger place in 
design as a whole.

Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide
What does a UX designer do?
Okay, so what are the responsibilities of the UX designer? 
The answer is – A large part of the role of a UX designer is 
Strategy and Content. 
Strategy and Content - Content is king on the 
web, and so strategizing how that content is 
applied and how it’s received best while 
interacting with the user. That plays a big role for 
the User Experience designer.

A UX designer will do a competitor analysis, a 
Customer Analysis and overall content structure 
and strategy, and helps to develop the content 
so it fits the best for users. User Experience 
design is a user first kind of discipline, where 
always user comes first. And by always means, 
ALWAYS. Another big responsibility of the UX 
designer would be Wireframing and Prototyping.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide
Wireframing and Prototyping – All of these 
elements are within the responsibility of a UX 
designer since they are attempting to determine 
what structure works best so that the user may 
receive or take in the material as needed.

So first of all, let’s understand Customer Experience (CX) in 
detail.
What is Customer Experience (CX)?
Customer Experience is a broader term gor all of the 
customers interactions with a firm with the specific product 
they have or any service they provide. It is the sum total of 
customers' perceptions and feelings resulting from 
interactions with a brand's products and services. Customer 
experience spans the lifetime of customers' relationships 
with a brand, starting before a purchase is made, 
continuing to active use and advancing to renewal or 
repeat purchase.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide
In simple words, CX is the sum-totality of how customers 
engage with your company and brand, not just in a 
snapshot of time, but throughout the entire arc of being a 
customery.
CX design responsibilities
In a majority of the organizations, the strategy of customer 
experience falls under the Marketing Umbrella. Being a CX 
designer, you have some responsibilities to follow. Below, 
I’ve mentioned some of the major responsibilities that can 
be seen or say followed by the CX designers.
Being focused on looking across all client 
engagement efforts and providing insights to 
help decision makers provide a better experience 
to both our client as well as the employees.
CX has a broader impact across the company – 
both internally as well as externally.
CX responsibilities can be assigned even to the 
Operations and Finance teams
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide
There’s no right answer to where your CX efforts should live, 
but the deciding factor should most likely be proximity to 
actual clients so that they can gain customer feedback in 
real time and have a better understanding of customer 
interactions. The closer your CX teams are to connecting 
directly with the people you serve, the better your CX will be 
and the more likely customer satisfaction and a positive 
experience is likely to occur when dealing with your 
organization.

your company's individuality. They can attract people and 
help you deliver your message more effectively. Colors are 
likely to be mentioned heavily in any UI Design standards.

Color may establish a brand's or product's basic mood, 
tone, concept, and meaning. According to research 
conducted by the Institute for Color, customers assess the 
quality of online purchases in roughly 90 seconds. On a 
subconscious level, color influences anywhere from 62 
percent to 90 percent of all product evaluations. As a result, 
picking the right colors for your logo, brand, and product 
packaging should never be done on the heat of a moment.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

For a better user experience, you can use a UX color palette. 
Better information readability is always aided by the use of 
the appropriate color scheme. It can also help to strengthen 
features like call-to-action buttons. It has the potential to 
improve customer navigation. It has the ability to meet the 
user's subconscious aesthetic needs. Finally, it can elicit 
intuitive responses.
As a result, the color of language might impact a buyer's 
selection. Color in user interface design is now an important 
aspect of any internet marketing plan.

Each UI color choice has a unique meaning and

Choose the right color in the proper pattern
Different colors in UI design communicate different 
concepts to the senses. In an ideal world, you'd pick the 
right one at the right moment and in the correct pattern.

They must be targeted to the right users, and you should 
choose them to achieve the right objectives.

## Sección 6: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.31]

Primary colors are those that cannot be produced by mixing 
two or more other colors. Red, blue, and yellow are the main 
colors.

The secondary colors are orange, purple, and green, which 
may be created by mixing any two of the three basic colors.

Tertiary colors are created when you combine a main and a 
secondary color. Magenta, vermillion, violet, teal, amber, 
and chartreuse are the tertiary hues.

The term "balance" is used in a variety of situations. Balance 
is typically thought to be a very positive thing, whether it's 
used to describe your nutrition, the legal system, or standing 
on your own two feet. Nobody wants to be out of balance in 
anything in the great majority of circumstances.

The same rule applies in the design world. It's important to 
strike a good balance.

Objects with the same physical weight will balance each 
other on a scale in the physical world. The distribution of 
visual weight is referred to as balance in design.
Pixsel Academy

Objects with the same physical weight will balance each

Visual weight
A basketball and a bowling ball look to weigh the same due

tells us otherwise. We know that a bowling ball is 
substantially heavier than a basketball in terms of physical 
weight.
However, in design, we are constrained by our perception. 
Because both things compete for our attention equally, the 
image above remains visually balanced despite their 
physical weight differences.

The perceived weight of an element in your design is known 
as visual weight. It's a metric for how distinctive an element 
is in comparison to its surroundings.

The size, color, contrast, and/or density of an element can 
all affect its visual weight.

weight, assuming everything else is equal.

Types of Balance
There are four techniques to establish design balance. 
Balance is usually achieved on two sides of an unseen axis, 
which might be vertical or horizontal.

This refers to your design's visual direction. While vertical 
and horizontal balance are the most common, it can also be 
achieved using diagonal or numerous axes.
1. Symmetrical/Formal Balance
With symmetrical balance, the visual weight is distributed 
evenly. You can draw a straight line through the middle of 
the design in any direction and the visual balance would be 
evenly distributed. This makes the composition appear 
stable and creates a more orderly look.

As you can see both sides of the composition carry the 
same visual rate. Neither side feels heavier than the other.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

 This is a perfectly balanced design.
However, look at the image below. As soon as you change 
the color of one of the sides to a light value while the other 
side remains a dark value, notice what happens. Doesn’t the 
dark value side feel heavier than the other side?
It’s important to keep in mind that while symmetrical 
balance is great and allows for viewer’s eye to get a 
stronger sense of what is being communicated, it doesn’t 
always relay an interesting design. Finding the center of the
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

2. Asymmetrical/Informal Balance
design and mirroring the weight on each side with various 
techniques will keep your design from being boring.
Visual balance doesn’t mean that every element has to be 
distributed with perfect symmetry. Balance can be achieved 
through asymmetry as well. You can think of it like the 
seesaw you might have played on when you were young, or 
as a beam balance scale. You can have different weights on 
each side, but can remain balanced by how the heavier and 
lighter elements are positioned and stacked.
An asymmetrical composition is intended to create a 
deliberate imbalance of the elements in the design. 
Asymmetricality can create tension and give your 
composition a sense of movement. To get this effect, one 
side can feel heavier than the other as long as it is still 
balanced. 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

For instance, you can have several small elements that 
balance out one large element. Or, you can have smaller 
elements positioned further away from the center of the 
composition. In either case, the elements are not the same 
size and not positioned evenly like with symmetrical 
balance. However, your composition still has a sense of 
balance while creating visual interest.

You can see a great example of asymmetrical balance in 
the image below. The elements on the left feel a bit heavier 
than the right, which create a perfect asymmetrical 
balance.
It can be more challenging to design in an asymmetrical 
way because you can’t just perfectly match each side’s 
visual weight. You will have to experiment with different 
ways to balance the elements to give it the right feel.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

3. Radial Balance
4. Mosaic/Crystallographic Balance
Rather than balancing both sides of a centered line, you can 
also choose to use radial balance around a single point (like 
a snowflake). Usually, this is done from the center, but not 
always.

Balance can be attained through placing objects, colors, or 
textures at equal distances from the center, or in the same 
way as balancing a seesaw. 
Sometimes called crystallographic balance, mosaic 
balance is a type of organized chaos. It may look like 
“noise” at first because of a distinct lack of focal point, but 
upon closer inspection you will find that it all works together 
when the elements share some type of uniform emphasis.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

An example of mosaic balance is a painting by Jackson 
Pollock.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

End Point
In UI design, you are required to keep everything balanced. 
And when everything is balanced, harmony is created. 
Maintaining a sense of uniformity in your overall design 
requires UI design balance. Visual strain can be caused by a 
loss of equilibrium, which should be avoided in most 
instances. Visual tension, on the other hand, can be exploited 
to accomplish a desired consequence if done correctly.

Alignment
According to the alignment principle, many objects are said 
to be aligned when their left or right edges, or center-lines, 
line up on a common area of the design.

Google Docs and Microsoft Office Suite's text align tools are 
two of the most common examples of alignment as a 
feature.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

The left alignment aligns the text's edges to the document's 
left side, whereas the centre and right alignments align the 
text to the document's centre and right sides, respectively.

Take a look at a table with rows and columns. To be 
connected, we connect the sentences or items in the same 
row or column. This is how alignment is utilized in UI design 
to make information easier to scan.
Why Alignment is important in design?
1. Alignment contributes to the aesthetics of the products. 
Aesthetics are important for creating first impressions. 
Alignment helps create designs that are more visually 
pleasing to users.

2. Alignment promotes proper product organization that 
helps users navigate easily through the product. Users 
prefer things that are organized. It helps them find what 
they are looking for.

3. Alignment creates a sense of unity in the product. Playing 
around with different types of alignment can create an
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

interesting outcome where we can unify or vary the design.

4. Alignment guides the users with design. Alignment helps 
the user to understand the connections better between 
different elements without the need for external helpers.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Types of Alignment:
1. Vertical Alignment
Vertical alignment is achieved when the centre, top, and/or 
bottom of items align on an unseen horizontal line.
Vertical alignment gets its name from the vertical space 
within the piece that is aligned with another, not from the 
invisible horizontal lines. When drawing a line from top to 
bottom, one key to remember is to think of the direction 
you're going.

A row of pieces need not always be vertically aligned by 
their tops, bottoms, and centre. Your elements will have 
variable heights in many circumstances, and you'll have to 
choose which one makes the most sense.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Top or Bottom Vertical Alignment
When the heights of two or more items are dramatically 
varied, vertically aligning them by their tops or bottoms 
might make sense.

The main body section and the sidebar in this example have 
a top vertical alignment.
In the example above, notice how both containers and their 
headings are aligned to establish a connection between 
both elements.
Centered-Vertical Alignment
When an element's height varies somewhat but not 
significantly, vertically aligning them across the centre may 
make more sense.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Each of the navigation components in the header has a 
centred vertical alignment in the example above.
2. Horizontal Alignment
Horizontal alignment is achieved when the centre, left side, 
and/or right side of items align on an unseen vertical line.

Consider the way you go when drawing a line from left to 
right to remember horizontal alignment.

Left and Right Horizontal Alignment
The simplest technique to assure left and right alignment 
when developing a layout is to set a max-width for all of 
your content from the start. Whether your pieces are pushed 
to the left or right, this invisible box will ensure that they are 
aligned.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Centered Horizontal Alignment
When you only have one element in a row, you can utilize 
centered horizontal alignment. Call to action text is the 
most prevalent application for this in UI design.
Text should, nevertheless, be aligned to the left in the 
majority of circumstances (if your audience reads from left 
to right). The eye jumps from one line to the next in a 
predictable manner when the eye is aligned to the left. After 
a few lines of text, readability starts to suffer with centred or 
right alignment, so keep this in mind when creating.
Media Object Alignment
Although media objects are very common UI Patterns, 
misalignment is easy to achieve with variable width icons.

Optical Alignment
Top, bottom, left, right, and centre alignment are not always 
the best options. You'll frequently come across scenarios 
where elements are precisely aligned, but do not appear to 
be, due to an element's uneven visual weight distribution.
In these circumstances, you'll have to modify the alignment 
with your eye to see what appears best. Optical Alignment 
is a term that refers to the process of aligning.

The final alignment type for text I want to touch upon is 
justified alignment. This is used primarily by print media. For 
justified alignment, we stick both left and right sides of the 
text paragraphs to their respective edges.

Newspaper articles primarily use justified texts because 
they implement multiple columns for their text composition. 
The justification works as the separator between the blocks

sentences can cause readers to easily lose track of which

discovered multiple times throughout history, as evidenced 
by its many titles, including golden mean, golden ratio, 
golden section, divine proportion (coined by Leonardo Da 
Vinci), and the Greek symbol φ.

By dividing each golden rectangle into a square and 
another golden rectangle, you can continue the procedure 
indefinitely. The result will be a golden spiral, which will be 
formed by connecting the squares' opposite corners.
To obtain a golden spiral, start by dividing a golden 
rectangle ABCD into a square ABEF and a smaller golden 
rectangle EFDC. Then repeat the process for the smaller 
golden rectangle EFDC. The golden spiral is formed by 
connecting the opposite corners of the resulting squares.

Many naturally occurring elements, such as flora and 
weather patterns like hurricanes, contain the golden spiral.

Examples of the Golden Ratio in UI Design
What are some examples of how the golden ratio is applied 
in interface design? Let's look at a number of common 
scenarios:
1. Text Sizes
A golden ratio can be achieved by using different font sizes. 
Let's say your website's body-font size is 16px. The size of the 
header font might be a golden-ratio multiple of your body 
size, such as 16φ = 16 x 1.618 = 25.88 or around 26px.

Some people use the golden ratio to figure out how much 
line height is required for a certain font size to be in a 
golden ratio. (The line height is the font height plus the white 
space between lines of text; it determines how densely the 
lines of text in a paragraph are ordered.) For example, if your 
body copy had a font size of 16px, the line height would be 
16φ = 16 x 1.618 = 25.88, or around 26px.

Webpage Layout
A 2-column layout is naturally created when you divide a 
golden rectangle into a square and a smaller golden 
rectangle. The resulting layout is perfect for a main content 
area and sidebar. Loosely following this layout creates 
structure and balance to an interface. However, on today’s 
responsive websites, it will be hard to fully support the 
golden ratio across all viewport sizes. If you focus on 
creating a basic grid based on the ratio and align 
components to it, you will be able to get the basic benefits 
of the ratio at least on some screen sizes.

What is Whitespace?
The area between design elements is known as white space. 
It also refers to the space between particular design 
elements, such as between typography glyphs (readable 
characters).

White space, despite its name, does not have to be white. 
Any color, texture, pattern, or even a backdrop image can 
be used.

Designers and clients may have issues with white space. 
White space is encouraged in design philosophy to add 
beauty and provide a good user experience. Unfortunately, 
many clients and supervisors regard white space as a waste 
of time. They believe it might be utilized to store additional 
information or graphic elements.

White space, on the other hand, is an excellent tool for 
balancing design features and better organizing content in 
order to enhance the visual communication experience. If 
you were a technical writer in charge of writing hundreds of
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

manuals for a kitchen appliance manufacturer, you'd strive 
for a perfect mix of words and graphics. Few people enjoy 
reading manuals, therefore it's critical to keep their 
attention! The white space, which works between the words 
and the photos, is the real star of the show in this case. It 
prevents each page from appearing cluttered.

Readers will read the troubleshooting information instead of 
calling the helpline. When people are bombarded with 
information, they become irritated. We're not machines; 
we're humans. White space relaxes us and allows us to 
"breathe."

You, as a designer, must understand the role white space 
plays in the user interface and experiment to establish the 
proper balance between it and the other aspects, just as 
you must with the other elements.
Macro vs Micro Whitespace
The density of micro white space and its relationship to 
macro white space are often used to categorize white 
space. We're discussing proportions and ratios.

## Sección 14: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.31]

Macro Whitespace
The enormous gap between main layout pieces, and the 
space surrounding the design layout, is referred to as macro 
white space. Macro white space can be found to the right 
and left of most websites' content, as well as in the space 
between content blocks.

Macro white space, unlike micro white space, serves as a 
container for the entire design. It's white space in the "large 
picture" that's easier to detect. The homepage of 
Google.com is probably the best example of a website that 
uses macro white space extensively (and efficiently). 
Although Google's trademark design is elegantly simple, 
have you noticed something else? It's also comforting

Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Micro Whitespace
The little gap between design elements is referred to as 
micro white space. It's located between lines and 
paragraphs. It comprises the space between grid pictures 
as well as the space between menu links.

The amount of micro white space in a piece of content has a 
direct impact on its legibility. Marginal white space around 
paragraphs, for example, has an impact on the user's 
reading speed and understanding.

People read more slowly when content appears in margins 
outside of regular paragraphs. They find it more difficult to 
comprehend than text with no margins.

because of its simplicity. There is less work for your eyes and 
mind since there is less clutter. You can concentrate on the 
purpose you've come; that is to find something.

There's a fascinating narrative behind how Google came to 
be in this position. One of the first websites to make great 
use of white space was Google.com. Internet connections 
were far slower when they first started. Despite the fact that 
everything was already on screen, users in user tests would 
wait for the page to download. They hadn't expected to see 
so much white space! Based on the results of these user 
tests, Google designers opted to place a copyright notice at 
the bottom of the page to let people know that the page 
has fully loaded.

So, what determines which white space you should use?

Well, the use of both macro and micro spaces depends on 
the following factors:
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Content: With more information in the layout, fewer 
amounts of macro white space will be available. 
Micro white space, on the other hand, will grow in 
volume. This balance is essential; otherwise, pages 
would be solid slabs of data, making reading 
incredibly difficult! Boulton (2007), for example, 
illustrates how news websites rely on micro white 
space to deliver a comprehensible experience for 
consumers. The reading comfort of users is critical.
Design: The layout's micro and macro white space 
ratio is influenced by the user interface design. The 
design style is chosen by the designer. This can lead 
to a preference for one form of white space over 
another in the layout.
User: User research (including demographic 
information) can be used to determine the right 
balance of macro and micro white spaces for that 
specific audience. There is no “rule of thumb” to 
apply consistently between audiences. As always in
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

user experience, we need to check with the target 
users of our product or service.
Branding message: The use of white space can 
indicate a company's budget and, as a result, the 
product's quality. Consider the branding of Apple, 
Mercedes-Benz, and IKEA, to name a few. What 
evidence do they have to back up this theory?

Active vs Passive: Another way to approach 
the usefulness of white space
Apart from the distinction between micro and macro white 
space, white space can also be classified as active or 
passive.
The white space use to improve page structure and lead the 
user through the information on the page.
It is used to improve the layout's attractiveness without 
directing the user through a specified reading, flow, or 
content order. The white space between font glyphs and 
paragraph lines for example, serves this purpose.

negative space
1. Legibility:
Micro white space, as we've seen, is critical for legibility of 
interface material. When selecting design typography 
characteristics such as font, size, color, style, leading, 
kerning, and tracking, designers should keep white space in 
mind. Do you recall the image on the previous page? The 
impact of changing the white space arrangement on 
reading performance and the overall user experience is 
significant. Readers that are happy are much more inclined 
to stay on your page.
The use of white space contributes to the overall design's 
tone. Larger amounts of macro white space on a website 
may convey minimalism and elegance. Websites with a 
smaller quantity of macro white space, such as news sites, 
may appear to be more informative (Kyrnin, 2015).

Proximity
Design elements that are close together are considered to 
be related, whereas elements that are separated are 
considered to belong to different groups.

Gestalt psychologists discovered Gestalt principles in the 
first half of the twentieth century while trying to understand 
how individuals visually see the environment, specifically 
how they decide whether certain elements belong to the 
same group or not. These principles include proximity, 
similarity, and closure, all of which are important in digital 
interface visual design. More grouping principles were later 
added to the initial Gestalt list (such as common region).
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Since accurately interpreting which items are connected is 
important in order to successfully engage with the interface, 
UI design strongly relies on proximity and other grouping 
principles.

Definition: The proximity principle states that items that are 
close together are more likely to be seen as belonging to the 
same group, with similar functions or characteristics.
Related Elements should be placed near 
each other

Far-Away Elements Appear Unrelated, Are 
Easily Overlooked
When consumers miss a link, button, or piece of information 
that is right in front of them, proximity is frequently to 
blame. Far-off objects might be easily ignored by taskfocused users who anticipate all relevant information and 
interactive elements to be positioned close together 
because things separated by whitespace are perceived as 
being less linked. Users selectively attend to certain areas of 
the screen as they complete their task, and miss things "in 
plain sight" because they are outside this focal area. This 
behaviour is sometimes referred to as "tunnel vision"

For example, In our mobile usability research, users are 
frequently frustrated when apps demand them to create an 
account before they can access content. However, in many 
of these designs, account creation can be skipped – this 
option is simply hidden in the upper corner of the page, far 
away from the major calls to action.

content. Furthermore, the photo background for the

Proximity may shift in responsive designs
When building flexible layouts, paying attention to the 
closeness of items is especially crucial, as these groupings 
may change as the layout adapts to different screen sizes. 
When elements are scaled down to smaller devices, the 
space between them is reduced or the elements are pushed 
further apart, disrupting grouping relationships.

For example, On the Transport for London Driving page on a
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

desktop, links to information on the Ultra Low Emission Zone

side. The near closeness of these two links allows users to 
easily observe and compare them before deciding which 
one they wish to click on. These links seem far apart on small 
screens because the two columns are placed one over the 
other rather than side by side. Because of this inconvenient 
positioning, mobile users may never see the second sort of 
emission zone.

What are patterns in UI design?

## Sección 17: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.445]

UI design patterns are repeating solutions to user interface 
design difficulties. They assist us avoid reinventing the 
wheel and speed up the process of developing a 
dependable solution to an interface design problem.

UI design patterns are not the brainchild of a designer, 
despite the fact that they are the bread and butter of UI 
design. They were created to optimise the user interface 
development process and are based on architecture and 
programming.

Developers were able to reduce the time they spent building 
and testing sections by creating a library of components 
that provide proven answers to user concerns, speeding up
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

the whole development process. We UI designers were first 
to recognise the advantages of UI design patterns.
Users are familiar with UI design patterns, which can be 
seen on a variety of websites and apps. An interface 
developed with standard UI design patterns will take a user 
seconds to learn what to do. Adopting common patterns 
allows you to take use of this expertise and make your 
product easier to use.

It's important to remember that UI design patterns aren't a 
one-size-fits-all solution; each pattern you employ will need 
to be customised for an unique use case.

T-shirts are worn by the majority of us. My t-shirt and yours 
may be different sizes and fits, but they're both t-shirts. We 
can add a handy small pocket, short sleeve detailing, and 
print all sorts of things on them. Regardless of how much we 
customise the garment to our desire, the structure remains 
essentially the same.

So basically, Design patterns for user interfaces are similar. 
They provide a general framework to which we can add,
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

remove, or adjust pieces to customise it to a specific set of 
needs specific to each design job.
Why are patterns in UI design important?
UI design patterns are visual techniques for rapidly and 
efficiently tackling common UI design challenges. They're 
not merely common elements that can be copied and 
pasted into an interface. Consider them a template that 
designers can refer to while building user interfaces. UI 
design patterns also give designers a shared vocabulary, 
reducing misunderstandings and ensuring consistency when 
numerous designers are working on the same project.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

User interface design patterns aren't simply useful for 
designers; they're also crucial for user retention. Common UI 
design patterns are the reason why so many apps and 
websites feel so user-friendly. Could you picture visiting a 
website without a navigation bar or any obvious buttons? 
Isn't it puzzling? When users become conditioned to viewing 
certain patterns, any variation from such patterns may 
result in the loss of important leads and conversions. By 
making the interface feel intuitive, UI design patterns 
reduce cognitive burden.
The UI design pattern formula
Online, you'll find a wide variety of UI design pattern 
libraries. You'll discover UI design patterns organized in 
them. They can assist you with a variety of difficulties, but 
the elements in each are the same.

The most common UI design patterns?
The following are some of the most common user interface 
design patterns:
Social Sharing: These patterns make it easier to 
promote and share content across different social 
media networks.

Navigation: Navigational patterns assist users in 
navigating the UI and ensure that they may return 
to the homepage if they become disoriented.

Input/Output: These patterns include a variety of 
forms that allow users to enter data or receive 
feedback from the site in response to a specific 
activity.

Content structuring: these patterns are concerned 
with how your site is organized in a way that feels 
intuitive and accessible.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

What are dark UI design patterns?
A dark pattern is a type of user interface that appears to 
have been carefully crafted to trick users into doing things 
that are not in their interest and is usually at their expense.
Some dark patterns, such luring users into signing up for emails, are less dangerous. Others obfuscate important 
details, causing customers to be tied into memberships or

How to use UI design patterns
UI design patterns can't be directly applied to a user 
interface. We'll have to customize them to match a certain 
case. So, how do we go about doing it?

Begin by defining the problem you're trying to solve. Go to 
your preferred UI design pattern library with a clear problem 
in mind. Select the category that best meets your 
requirements.

Take a look at all of the different samples provided. What is 
the intended application of the pattern? What other 
designers have done with it? What lessons can you take 
away from it? Pay attention to the many types of elements 
and how they are organized.

Perception
Our vision allows us to process information from the 
environment around us, which is transmitted to our eyes via 
light. The process of our senses gathering up and conveying 
information from the world around us is known as sensation. 
The process through which the brain organizes and 
interprets data received from sense organs – in the case of 
visual perception, the eyes – is known as perception. 
Culture, past experiences, and our expectations all 
influence how we perceive things.
Seeing is not believing
What should designers be aware of when it comes to 
perception? It's helpful to understand some of the processes 
that influence how people experience and perceive our 
designs when we create for them. Perception is a personal 
and complex process, and we will never be able to fully 
control how people perceive things. However, we can 
improve how we design to accommodate for some of the 
psychological bases of perception.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

The first thing to remember is that seeing isn't the same as 
believing. Our perception, particularly our visual vision, can 
be manipulated in a variety of ways. You may recall the 
frustrating nature of optical illusions, which illustrate the 
following point.
Our cultural background has an impact on what we'see,' for 
example, variances in depth perception have led to the 
'carpentered world' theory. According to this concept, we 
are more prone to illusions like the Muller-Lyer illusion when 
we are used to live in an environment full of straight lines 
and right angles. Europeans and Americans were shown to 
be the most sensitive to the illusion in a classic 1963 study by
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Segal et al, whereas Kalahari hunter-gatherers were the 
least susceptible. The important takeaway is that what we 
perceive is influenced by our material culture and what we 
see in the constructed environment around us.

It's critical for designers to understand how culture affects 
perception, and they should test with their target audience 
to learn more.
Great Rule of Thumb – Gestalt Principles
What do we know about how to alter perception if it is a 
complex and subtle process?

In the 1930s and 1940s, a group of psychologists formulated 
some core theories on visual perception. The core idea of 
gestalt psychology is that the mind tries to make a 'unified 
whole' (Gestalt) out of the fragmented and chaotic 
information it receives. "The whole is other than the sum of 
the parts," said Gestalt psychologist Kurt Koffka.

From these studies, a set of foundational principles of visual 
perception developed. 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

These principles are fundamental to communicating visually 
in graphic and interface/web design. Understanding and 
applying gestalt principles can strengthen any designer’s 
work.
If you're like most people, the first thing that comes to mind 
is a triangle. But all that's there are three "pac guys" in 
actuality. We see the triangle because our brains organise 
the ambiguous visual data into something that makes sense 
to us—something familiar, organised, symmetrical, and 
understandable.

When this cognitive process kicks in, our minds go from 
understanding all of the elements as separate and

UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Consider that quote for a moment. How do people react to 
your designs when they first see them? To comprehend how 
UI design works, you must first comprehend the psychology 
of human perception.

A Gestalt principle's underlying law is that humans tend to 
order our experiences in a regular, orderly, and identifiable 
fashion. This is what enables us to find meaning in a chaotic 
and complex world. And having a firm grasp on how these 
concepts operate can benefit you in three ways:
They'll assist you in determining which design 
aspects work best in a certain setting. When to 
utilize visual hierarchy, background shading, and

These psychological concepts have the ability to

persuade us to do specific actions, and modify our 
behaviour.

Finally, the Gestalt principles assist you in designing 
goods that solve a customer's problem or meet a 
user's need in a way that is both beautiful and 
intuitive to use.
Gestalt Principles and its examples:
Now, we’ll go through seven Gestalt laws or principles that 
are directly applicable to modern design, as well as some 
examples of how they're employed in user interface design. 
This isn't a full list, but you'll note that many of the principles 
overlap and that they all operate together in a seamless 
manner. Let's get started. 
1. Figure-Ground
According to the figure-ground principle, people intuitively 
view objects as being in the foreground or background. They 
either stand out in the front (the figure) or fade into the 
background (the text) (the ground).

You’ve probably seen examples of this principle floating 
around in memes on social media, or as part of logos.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

In most circumstances, this decision will be made fast and 
subconsciously. In a composition, Figure - Ground tells us 
what we should pay attention to and what we can safely 
disregard or quite leave.

One of the first things visitors to your website or mobile app 
do on each screen is figure out which one is the figure and 
which one is the ground.

As an example we can see here that graphics, text, forms, 
and other information abound on the Basecamp 
homepage.

You can tell right away that you should concentrate on the 
material in the white foreground sections because of the 
figure-ground principle.

Establishing Visual Hierarchy 
When we look at posters, websites, or app screens, we 
interpret elements as having varying degrees of relative 
value. This is referred to as 'visual hierarchy,' and it describes 
how we scan information to determine which components 
are more or less significant. Because it gives clues 
surrounding what we want people to focus on, changing 
visual hierarchy is a vital approach for designers to produce 
a positive user experience. Visual hierarchy is influenced by 
the contrast of distinct parts, as well as gestalt principles.

The hierarchy will be determined by the flow and order of

in a Western cultural setting. Consider how the difference 
between a h1 and a h4 affects hierarchy when decorating 
HTML! The way we see hierarchy can also be influenced by 
color and shape. When it comes to color, this is exemplified 
by the notification bubble on the iOS home screen.

different illumination circumstances."

people with colorblindness). We want to make sure that our 
design works for everyone, even if they have different color 
experiences and perceptions than we have.

Design Basics

Icons
Icons are visual icons that are most commonly used to 
represent items and actions that users may interact with or 
manipulate. Icons can be displayed individually on a 
desktop or in a window, or in a toolbar. A warning symbol in 
a dialogue message box is an example of a secondary use 
of an icon to reinforce essential information.
Types of Icons
Humans have long used icons to represent objects, ideas, 
and actions. We've been in that situation previously. 
Pictographs and then ideographs were used to 
communicate by early people (about 100,000 years ago).

Some of these early communications can still be seen on 
cave walls and rock walls all around the world. In certain 
societies (for example, Native Americans and Australian 
aborigines), this was also the only way to communicate until

Chinese writing dates back to around 6000 B.C., and 
Egyptian hieroglyphics date back to around 3000 B.C. 
Cuneiform (Babylonia and Assyria) appeared circa 1900 
B.C., followed by the current Chinese vocabulary 
(numbering around 50,000) around 1500 B.C.

The Phoenicians devised a 22-sign alphabet in 1000 B.C., 
which the Greeks adopted between 800 and 600 B.C. 
Around 400 B.C., the Greeks passed this alphabet on to the 
Romans, who established a 23-character alphabet.

For the previous 2000 years, this alphabet has been 
updated and embellished, yet it has stayed essentially the 
same.

As a result, pictorial representations have played a

Glyph icons are graphical symbols that are usually solid, and 
their name comes from the Greek word for "carving." They 
may be altered with multiple colours and shadow effects and 
resized to any size you require.

Glyph icons function well at small sizes because they're 
usually solid colours, but they may lack visual interest at 
higher sizes.
1. Glyph Icons
2. Colored Icons
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Colored icons are exactly what they sound like: they're 
coloured. They can be solid or gradient in colour, and they 
can make symbols appear less official and more fanciful. 

Colored icons have the disadvantage of being more difficult 
to integrate into a product's style, and they can even 
distract consumers from important material. As a result, 
excessive use is often bad for UX.
While most coloured icons utilise only one colour (or a 
gradient), duotone icons employ two unique colours that are 
related but not identical. To make one, divide an icon's 
elements into two layers (such as an outline and fill area), 
choose a starting colour, and play around with layer opacity 
until you have a duotone icon.
3. Duotone Icons
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Vector strokes are used to make outlined icons, which are 
empty on the inside. They have advantages and 
disadvantages. On the plus side, they're simple, minimalist, 
and can appear quite professional. On the other hand, they 
may take longer for users to process and recognise.
Duotone icons can give your designs a little more visual 
appeal without overwhelming them. Just keep in mind that 
they can be difficult to read at very small sizes.
4. Outlined Icons
5. Universal Icons
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Universal icons are instantly identifiable and usually 
symbolise operations that are repeated frequently, such as 
home, print, or search. To avoid confusion, universal actions 
in your product should be represented by universal symbols.

The search icon, for example, is generally a magnifying 
glass. Trying to be innovative by employing binoculars 
instead will simply confuse users. While you can change the 
icon's exact style to meet your design, the core form and 
concept should remain the same.
6. Unique Icons
Unique icons, as opposed to universal symbols, symbolise 
specific functions or features. The disadvantage of 
employing them is that they can be difficult to understand for 
first-time users. If you utilise them, be sure to add text labels 
so that consumers don't have to figure out what they signify.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Like
One example of unique icons is logo icons. A stylised 
diamond serves as the Sketch logo. The diamond will be 
meaningless to someone who is inexperienced with the 
programme. Those who have used the product, however, will 
recognise it right away. You have to keep this in mind while 
using one-of-a-kind icons.
7. Conflicting Icons
In some circumstances, you'll have to choose between 
several icons that all reflect the same idea. Conflicting icons 
are what they're called.

To like a post on Facebook, for example, you'd use a thumbs 
up, while on Instagram, you'd use a heart, and on other sites, 
you'd use a star. While any of those icons can be used in 
your design, the key is to be consistent and choose only one 
for your interface taht suits the best for it.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

On different websites, conflicting icons can have different 
meanings. Returning to the heart icon, it likes a post on 
Instagram, but it also likes a post on Facebook, and it adds 
a product to your favourites list on sites like Spotify. That 
isn't to say you shouldn't use a heart symbol; it just means 
you should use it with caution.
You should know the anatomy of an icon now that you've 
learned about the many types of icons. This is useful while 
creating your own icons, but it's also useful when 
determining the compatibility of an existing icon set you're 
considering to choose.

keylines provide a more detailed direction. The keylines are 
a kind of icon-making starter kit that includes a few basic 
forms like a square, rectangle, and circle. They assist you in 
maintaining consistent aesthetic proportions in your icon 
designs while allowing you to express yourself freely.

Another factor to consider when generating visually 
consistent icons is the border radius (the amount of sharp or 
rounded corners). If one icon has crisp, sharp corners, you'll 
want to make sure they're present in all of your other icons.

Example principles from the Phosphor icon family:
Clarity: Be clear first and foremost. Make the icon 
recognizable and readable. Never sacrifice clarity 
of what the icon represents.
Brevity: Use as few details as possible. Phosphor’s 
style is reductive. Be concise and intentional with 
every stroke to communicate the essence of what’s 
being represented.

you in creating more professional-looking designs.
A Little Design History Trivia
Before the age of computers, layout grids were 
drawn on mockups with blue pen. Every single line of 
the grid had to be measured and drawn by hand 
with T-rulers. If designers wanted to use a standard 
grid, they could use grid templates printed in blue 
ink that were placed underneath thick tracing 
paper.
The designer then set up their layout on top. Images 
were drawn as rectangles or squares with two crisscross lines from corner to corner. 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Computer based layout editors mirror the layout 
grid design practices from years ago. Blue ink was 
used because it didn’t show up on the pre-print 
films.
What is a Grid Layout design?
A grid is a collection of intersecting horizontal and vertical 
lines that split your mobile apps or webpage designs pages 
into few columns and rows. A grid, whether real or imagined, 
always serves as a foundation or backbone for positioning, 
aligning, and arranging material on your page with greater 
precision.

Reponsive Grid Layout
A typical question among UI designers is, "What is 
responsive grid layout, and how can I utilise it to better my 
design?" So, let's have a look at this grid.

The term "responsive grid layout" refers to a grid that can 
adjust to multiple screen sizes. The responsive grid layout, 
as opposed to the fixed grid layout, which can only be 
viewed on one device, allows you to read page content on a 
variety of devices and platforms. For designers that wish to

Fixed Behaviour
The container width and position are "fixed" in this 
behaviour. The container maintains all of its exact 
measurements while the screen size changes, but the 
margin size rises or decreases.

This keeps all parts proportional, however on extra-large 
device sizes, it can result in too much vacant space in the 
margins.

Fluid Behaviour
The container width changes as the screen size changes 
because fluid layouts are measured in percentages rather 
than pixels. 

While the column size increases or lowers, the container 
width adjusts by keeping the margin and gutter widths 
constant. 

This behaviour makes use of available screen space, 
although items may appear stretched out as a result.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Breakpoints
Designers and developers can easily adjust the layout of a 
design as it goes up from mobile to desktop using 
breakpoints. This allows us to adapt our designs to a variety 
of device sizes without sacrificing the user experience. 

Establishing a set of breakpoints makes it easy to express 
how designs should behave across devices of various sizes.

Breakpoints are commonly represented in “px” units and 
occur in ranges. While there isn't a "must-have" set of 
breakpoints, here are several to get you started.
660
1024
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

When demonstrating responsive design behaviour, we use 
the smallest device size in a particular breakpoint's range, 
with the exception of the "Small" breakpoint.
Modified layouts for each breakpoint can differ little (e.g., 
increasing gutter size) or significantly (e.g., increasing font 
size) (ie. changing column count, content layout, and UI 
elements used).

A responsive interface with fluid behaviour is seen below. 
Each layout expands until it reaches a breakpoint, after 
which it changes to the next breakpoint size. When the tiny 
layout reaches 600 pixels, it switches to the medium layout.

Designing with Grids in Figma
Because of its use of nested frames, styles, constraints, and 
customizability, Figma is one of the most powerful and 
flexible tools for working with layout grids.
Apply layout grid to any frame
Grids can be added to any frame in Figma, rather than just 
the artboard. This means that grids can be applied to both 
top-level and nested frames (desktop, tablet, and mobile) 
(ie. framed content areas or components within your 
design).

Apply Constraints for Responsive Designs
You can use constraints to govern how any element resizes 
in reference to its parent frame. You can pin an element 
(such as a button) to the corner of a frame, for example, and 
keep the precise size and padding when the frame is 
expanded or contracted.

restrictions can help you not just see your work from a 
different perspective, but also develop better solutions.

Why do you need good typography?
Typography is more than just a visual communication tool 
when it comes to building a website or mobile app. It also 
has a number of advantages:
1. Enhance UI readability, legibility and accessibility
Good typography makes it easier for people to read, scan, 
and move around a page by delivering content in an 
immersive visual manner. It shortens the time it takes for 
people to find the information they need and understand 
how the product or service can help them.

2. Set a consistent tone and mood
The general tone or atmosphere of all interfaces is 
sometimes established by the typeface style. When you 
show the page banners or headlines with a cartoon font 
with warm colours, soft lines, and bold strokes, for example, 
you add fun and personalization to your page. When you 
select a plain, simple, and serious typography, though, you 
appear to be setting a serious tone for your entire 
programme.

3. Make your app stand out from others
High-quality typography, like other aspects in UI design, 
frequently has a professional appearance and blends the 
brand vision, colour, and concept. All of these qualities help 
you stand out from the crowd. However, customers are 
impressed by unusual and excellent typography, which 
helps them recall the website or mobile app brand.
4. Boost product sales
Successful typographic design encourages users to spend 
more time on your app and makes it simple for them to 
easily access the information they require. This boosts 
product or service sales by removing any customer 
distractions.

There are a variety of typographic styles that can help you 
convey your message more effectively. Italic and bold are 
the most frequent typeface styles. In any graphic design 
editor, regular typefaces can be made bold or italic. 
Headlines and highlighting crucial areas of text benefit from 
bold styles. The use of bold styles for links makes them out 
and is easier to see. Italics are mainly used for words that 
are written in a different language or for reference links.
Typeface Families
Some typefaces provide more alternatives than others. A 
typeface family might contain up to 20 different styles. 
Futura is a typeface family with 22 different styles. Light, 
condensed, semi-bold, extra bold, bold condensed, and 
book italic are some of the most notable Futura styles.

options than novelty styles. They convey so many messages 
that using them in your design will immediately give it a 
preconceived connotation, therefore use caution while 
utilising these fonts.

How Type is Manipulated to Fit in a Space
Letters may need to be resized to fit a certain space. There 
are three distinct ways to move type around in a space to 
improve its appearance. Kerning, tracking, and leading are 
the terms for the below given three metrics.
The space between two individual letters is known as 
kerning. It's utilised when only one letter has to be moved 
because it's too far away or too close to its neighbours. The 
space between the capital letter and the rest of the word 
seems odd in some typefaces.

Kerning aids in the creation of a better letter-to-letter 
balance.

Designers generally regulate the kerning from letter to letter 
while creating wordmark logos, ensuring that the entire 
word is precisely balanced and polished.
The proportionate gap between all the letters in a piece of 
text is known as tracking. Changing the tracking allows you 
to fit more letters in a short space or stretch out letters that 
are too close together. Due to the way ligatures split and 
produce imbalanced spaces, script fonts cannot be tracked 
too much.

When designers want to get a look with even borders for all 
of the words, they alter the tracking. They can make all of 
the text look coherent and justified by spreading out or

Buttons in General
Buttons vs Links
Buttons indicate what activities users can do. They're 
usually seen in places like Dialogs, Forms, and Toolbars in 
your user interface. It's important to understand the 
difference between buttons and links:
Links are used to navigate to another location, such as the 
"see all" page, the "Roger Wright" profile, and so on.
Link
Buttons are used to accomplish actions like "submit," 
"merge," "make new," "upload," and so on.
Use on actions that won’t affect the 
website at all

by its state.

the right interactions and styles for your buttons. Each state 
must have distinct affordances that identify it from the 
other states and the surrounding layout, but they should not 
significantly modify a component or cause excessive visual 
noise.

Object and Shapes
Intro
When it comes to expressing brand and hierarchy in user 
interfaces, designers frequently use well-known colour, 
graphics, and font systems. But there's another item in the 
designer's toolbox that's often overlooked: Object and 
Shape.

Shape analysis can be applied to any visual objects. A 
typical house, for example, may be regarded as a rectangle 
with a triangle on top, while the sun is frequently depicted 
as a circle with lines surrounding it.

When most people hear the word shape, they immediately 
think of geometric forms. People observe a variety of 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

geometric shapes on a daily basis, including squares, 
circles, rectangles, and other shapes. But what exactly do 
they imply?

Just keep on reading, below is the answer to every of your 
questions. 
Know your Shapes and Objects
The user's attention is a valuable resource. Every second 
counts when it comes to site design/UI Design. How can you 
make sure your visitors see what you want them to see? 
What is the best way to call attention to a specific element? 
The solution is simple: experiment with visual weight.
What is Visual Weight?
Visual weight is a force that draws a viewer's attention. 
Every object on the page carries a certain amount of 
weight. The more weight an object carries, the more 
attention it attracts. We can use visual weight to generate 
focal points (also known as visual magnets or points of 
attraction) – things with more visual weight than others.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

How can you measure visual weight?
How are the images created?
Visual weight is a measure of how much something draws 
the viewer's attention, yet unlike physical weight, it cannot 
be quantified. However, a simple test may be used to 
determine which objects on a page have greater visual 
weight than others. Take a few seconds to look at a page or 
screen. Close your eyes for a moment. Try to recall which 
thing (or objects) from the page are still fresh in your mind. 
Those items will be more visually significant than others.
You'll use shapes and objects in a design programme of your 
choosing to create a low-fidelity wireframe or a completely 
finished, gorgeous UI design. You can even use Keynote or 
Powerpoint to familiarise yourself with forms and their 
relationships before moving on to Sketch, Figma, Adobe XD, 
or other programmes.

Vector shapes are used by all currently accessible UI design 
tools. That is to say, everything you see is represented by 
numbers. It also means that the item may be extended and

enlarged without losing quality, as it merely enlarges the 
numbers and recreates the object.

majority of your time pushing rectangular items about. 
Rectangles are the most common shape in all interfaces 
(right after ellipses).

Moving rectangles about in just the correct way is the 
general notion behind UI design.

The Box Model
In both design and programming, the Box model is the most 
fundamental approach of designing a digital interface 
object. The box model is used in the majority of the design 
work we undertake, therefore it's critical to understand it 
thoroughly.

Fill : The term "fill" should be self-explanatory. It's

Border : Our thing is surrounded by a border. In the 
following stories in this series, we'll look at all of the 
different types of boundaries.
The Outer Margin : The area outside of our object is 
known as the outer margin (or simply "margin"). It 
enables us to arrange it in a secure manner in 
relation to everything around it. It's the blue space 
on the outskirts of our rectangle.
The Inner Margin : The inner margin is also known 
as padding, which may be useful when speaking

area inside our colorful rectangle in the illustration

Size

(in points). W and H are the most common abbreviations. 
The width is a scale on the horizontal axis, while the height is 
a scale on the vertical axis, as it is a two-dimensional space.
A rectangle's width and height are measured in points.

Even if the object has an irregular shape, the width and 
height are always of the smallest available rectangular box 
that can accommodate it.

Angle 
Angle determines the clockwise rotation of our object, with 
0° being the default. Rotation can be a negative number as 
well. It's worth noting that a -15° rotation is equivalent to 
360° - 15° = 345°.
It's advisable not to rotate things by hand in order to 
maintain a consistent interface; instead, enter the proper 
angle from the keyboard, which is far more precise than 
manually turning the item point by point.

If we Flatten the object (there's a button for that), the angle 
is reset to 0, but the object is still rotated — just the new 
perspective becomes the new rotation base.

Personal Interest
The personal interests of viewers have an impact on how 
they perceive design. Some topics pique our curiosity more 
than others. If we're more interested in trains than cars, for 
example, the image of a train will grab our attention when

various interests, two people viewing the same page can

How to Use Shapes in UI Design
Almost every visual object that you see can be analyzed in 
terms of shapes. Vector shapes are used by all currently 
accessible UI design tools. That is, the forms themselves are 
defined by numbers and may be extended and increased 
without losing quality. Their parameters are simply numbers 
that, with each modification, form a new shape based on 
the criteria.
Curves
A smooth-flowing continuous line that has bent is referred 
to as a curve. There are no sharp curves in it. The curve can 
be recognised by the fact that the line bends and changes 
direction at least once.

Two-dimensional curved shapes include circles, ellipses, 
parabolas, and hyperbolas, as well as arcs, sectors, and 
segments. Curves will look great on modern, user-friendly 
websites. But use them with caution.

modern, user-friendly websites. But use them with 
caution. Make sure you don't overdo it with them.

moving point creates. Thin or thick lines might be used. They 
might be short or lengthy. They might be horizontal, vertical,

Intro
The size, complexity, substance, and purpose of data tables 
varies. Regardless of the use case, well-designed data 
tables provide clarity on the data presented and assist users 
in making decisions and taking action.

This chapter contains a list of best practices for creating 
better data tables in your apps. Keep on reading and you’ll 
get each detailed information on building a table for your UI 
design.

What is the purpose of the Table?
In the world of digital interfaces, tables have a somewhat 
(undeserved) poor rep. They are frequently regarded as cold 
and intimidating. They are, however, the most effective 
approach to organize complex information in a digestible 
manner in many circumstances. In corporate software 
design, a well-thought-out table interface experience can 
improve clarity, make users' life easier, and optimise the 
data's value.

Large amounts of data are frequently served up to 
consumers by enterprise software businesses, therefore 
well-designed table experiences are a top focus.
Points to be remebered
There are a few things to ask about when developing a table 
interface. Here are a few points to get you started:
Type of data that will be contained in the cells.
You must first determine whether your data consists 
primarily of short numerical strings such as dates and sums,
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

or whether it contains more complicated data such as 
paragraphs of text, links, and files.
The user’s intended use for the table.
Types of devices on which the tables will be viewed 
on.
Does the table need to offer customizable viewing 
options?
The user experience of a table might vary dramatically 
depending on whether users anticipate to alter the data to 
read and compare information in order to extract insights or 
whether they intend to edit numbers, update statuses, and 
assign people.
The engagement experience on a mobile device and a huge 
desktop monitor can be very different.
This refers to how much assistance you want to give users in 
finding what they want to view. This can take a variety of 
forms, including searching, filtering, sorting, and altering 
the display density, among others.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

We're ready to go into some of the best practices for 
business table UI design now that you have a better notion 
of what the end experience should feel like. Be prepared to 
become engrossed in the tiniest of details that will allow this 
to all come together into a lovely experience.
Elements of Table
A table is made up of three components when broken down 
to its simplest form. Data, columns, and rows are all present. 
By juggling all that can be done with those elements and 
finding the appropriate balance for your specific corporate 
software needs, you can achieve peak table interaction.
Types of data
The data that a table is supposed to display is a crucial 
component. Whether the data is textual (strings, links, 
paragraphs) or numerical (amounts, dates, percentages), it 
requires special attention in order to provide the best 
possible experience. We've compiled a list of some of the 
best practises here.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Text
Left-align text columns

Everything made up of letters should be oriented to the left. 
This is how Western minds are accustomed to thinking. Let's 
not reinvent the wheel here; we've been reading words from 
left to right since childhood.
Match heading alignment to column

Column names (also known as headers, heads, or titles) 
should always correspond with the text of the column. This 
rule is broken, resulting in distracting whitespaces and 
superfluous visual noise.

## Sección 42: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.295]

It's crucial to employ the proper alignment for the right type 
of text if you want to improve the user's reading, mental 
calculations, and row comparisons. The eye jumps about 
excessively as a result of center alignment, which limits 
quick scanning and identifying abnormalities.
Avoid duplication

Avoid repeating the headline in every cell of a column if at 
all possible. For example, in cells like "Qualified Lead" or 
"Nurturing Lead," you can skip repeating the word "lead." 
Visual noise can be reduced by using the word "Lead" in the 
headline and only using qualifiers in the rows.

Numbers
Right-align numeric columns

When numerical values are right-aligned, they're 
considerably easier to compare and contrast than when 
they're left-aligned. The purpose is to align the numbers 
according to the decimal position. Aligning numbers to the 
right of the cell is the most sensible way to display them if 
you've properly prepared your data such that they all have 
the same number of decimal digits.

Use tabular typography

For numerical numbers, a monospace typeface is strongly 
recommended. Because all characters have the same size 
instead of having proportional spacing (where "W" is wider 
than "I" or "9" is broader than "1"), this form of typography is 
more easier to read and compare. This avoids issues like 
$1,111.11 appearing to be smaller than $999.99.
Some exceptions

If the first column has a date written in digits, left-align is 
advised. Although dates are technically numbers, they 
aren't really numerical values, thus it seems strange that the 
first column should be positioned to the right. To 
accommodate such a situation, all qualitative numbers can 
be adjustable. Some instances are as follows:

grey colour and a very tiny border of no more than 1px.

Grid

The largest separation between data points is achieved by 
using both horizontal and vertical lines, but the extra visual 
noise might be distracting. This spreadsheet-style is best for 
tables with a lot of data.
Horizontal lines

The visual noise of a full grid is reduced by only presenting 
horizontal lines. This design uses a lot of white space while 
yet allowing the reader to keep track of where they are in 
the book. This is the most popular and recommended 
approach for all data sets of any size.

Depending on the sort of table you're working with, allowing

of the rows is linked to scrolling, and you have very high rows

Sticky Headers & Footers
A sticky header is an excellent approach to keep the user in 
context while allowing them to effortlessly navigate 
throughout the table. The control panel should be included 
in that fixed component for tables with actions presented in 
a control panel row.
You can also make the controls appear only once one or 
more row items have been selected. They can be displayed 
as a toolbar at the bottom of the window, allowing the user 
to maintain context and readily understand what they're 
doing.

Pagination 
Sorting, Filtering & Searching
Make it clear to your users how many rows they're looking at 
from a universe count. To fine-tune decisions like "what 
should be the default view of the table," you should conduct 
usability testing on your tables. Users almost always 
navigated to the dropdown to pick "see all" in my usability 
testing on tables. 

As a result, I found it to be more user pleasant to set the 
default to show all of the data and include the number of 
lines in the table. A viewport's height and breadth only allow 
the user to see so much at once. For the table, add a simple 
horizontal and vertical scroll.

Filtering and searching are both rather difficult UI patterns 
in and of themselves, but they offer a lot of interaction 
potential in a table UI. We're still working on individual 
components on filtering and searching, so you’ll receive 
everything in detail about these topics real soon on our

At the very least, you should include basic options such as 
column sorting. It might be as basic as a small chevron next 
to the column headings to get a quick win with big rewards. 
This is another common occurrence, but there are a few 
things to keep in mind:

Make sure your default makes sense for the data you're 
dealing with. In most circumstances, default sorting places 
the most recent entries (those that have been created or 
edited recently) or those that require the most attention at 
the top (lowest inventory, most urgent priority).

The sort chevron should not obstruct the alignment of the 
heading in relation to the content of the column.

your UI table: 
 Alphabetical (A-Z) 
 Alphabetical (Z-A) 
 By recency (Oldest to newest) 
 By recency (Newest to oldest) 
 By size (Largest to smallest) 
 By size (Smallest to largest)

Row details
When the entire row becomes clickable when hovered over, 
it suggests that there are additional details in a secondary 
view.

It's possible that your rows just include generic information 
about the entries, but that more qualitative information is 
required. What's the best approach to show more row 
information within a table UI? It relies on your development 
resources as well as the amount of data that needs to be 
displayed. Let's have a look at some of the possibilities:

Expandable rows (Inline)
Although this is a more technically expensive alternative, 
having clickable rows expand inline is a natural method to 
convey more information. You're leaving blank space for 
additional precise details to live inside or outside the 
confines of the columns by doing this.

Multi-select & bulk actions
When a checkbox appears when hovering over a row, it 
indicates that the row is actionable.

'Delete,' 'Export,' 'Duplicate,' and whatever other 
functionality you desire to give your users are examples of 
such actions.

Only when one or more rows have been selected is it 
appropriate to display the actions. This is an excellent use of 
space that helps to limit the amount of clutter on the page.

Editing
When a cell is made to appear clickable when hovered over 
with a text cursor, it indicates that the content is editable.

Allowing adjustments to be made inline, i.e. in the original 
table view, reduces friction and preserves context; the user 
will still be able to see neighbouring rows and columns and 
will only require a few clicks to get started.

Forms
The majority of consumers would be unable to articulate 
what constitutes a good shape. Bad form design, on the 
other hand, is something we all identify when we see it.

We're all used to filling out forms these days, whether it's to 
connect to social media, check out of our favourite online 
stores, or hunt for a cheap airline for our next holiday. 
Because online forms are so popular, there is a lot of 
discussion over what characterizes a good form design.

While designing the form, there are many questions like, Is it 
because the form is so short? Is it the visual elements and 
how they're arranged that's the problem? Is it the time it

What is a Form?
A form is a set of questions that employ field entries to 
collect information. With that said, there are many different 
types of forms and roles that each one serves.

Some forms, such as login or checkout forms, are short and 
serve as a basic means to an end. Others, such as customer 
feedback surveys or event registration forms, deal with 
complex issues and require more questions to collect all of 
the essential data.
Key Principles
Because there are so many different sorts of forms out there 
in cyberspace, it's only logical that designers will have some 
flexibility when it comes to creating a form that is both 
creative and functional. When it comes to usability criteria, 
form design can be quite stringent, yet designers do have 
some freedom to make their forms memorable.

Regardless of whether you wish to make something unique, 
there are some fundamental principles that apply to all
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

forms. Key ideas that have a significant impact on the 
usability and user experience of your form, regardless of its 
shape or style.
Data Gathering
Data might be difficult to work with. When asking 
consumers for data, it's critical to have a clear picture of the 
problem or issue you're trying to solve – so you know exactly 
what kind of information you'll need.

It's pointless to inquire about a user's workout routine if you 
want to know how they feel about the design of your online 
store.

Requesting superfluous data not only adds to your workload 
(both in terms of form design and data processing), but it 
also adds to the workload of your users. Less is more when it 
comes to form design.

Apart from having a clear understanding of what you need 
to ask, you also want to make sure you ask it correctly. And 
by that, we mean that a question can be ambiguous 
depending on how it is phrased or framed.

Ambiguous questions allow the user to respond with 
multiple interpretations, which makes data analysis a 
nightmare.

The concept of ensuring that data means what it says is 
known as data integrity. It implies that your data must be 
precise and consistent, so that no one has to second-guess 
their own database.

In its most basic form, a form is a 
straightforward method to collect data 
that you may use for various purposes; if 
the data integrity is compromised, the 
form has failed to achieve its primary 
goal.“

Conversion
The primary purpose of any platform's form is to request 
information. However, in form design, the concept of data 
collection is linked to conversion. This is because the 
conversion rate will show how many people actually finished 
the form compared to how many people started it.

The conversion rate is an important indicator in all forms 
since it tells you if your design is asking too much of your 
visitors. In general, the quality and usefulness of your form's 
design will determine its conversion rate.

## Sección 47: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.265]

When given the opportunity, most users will abandon a form 
that appears to be excessively complicated and seek for a 
simpler option. To reduce this effect, you should reduce the 
cognitive work users must engage in the form by breaking it 
down to the bare minimum - but we'll get to that in the 
recommendations portion of this tutorial.
Interaction Cost
Forms are hard work for both designers and users, as we all 
know. Designers put in a lot of time and effort to create the 
final form design, which is normally done to increase the 
form's usability. The work for the user comes from both the 
mental and physical effort required to finish the file.

Granted, physical labour is usually light - but you'd be 
amazed. People can get tired of scrolling or clicking, 
especially if they don't see any progress in their assignment 
as a result of their efforts. This frequent checking to see if 
they're getting close to the finish of the form adds to the 
user's mental load. 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Here are some of the things users regularly experience 
online that push the interaction cost to the skies – in a really, 
really bad way:

Having to look around for information
Typing and scrolling
Having to wait for pages or files to load
Having to insert information more than once due to 
errors/mistakes
