Interaction cost is a key consideration in form design, and 
you want to keep it as low as possible. You may reduce the 
interaction cost by eliminating ambiguity and unnecessarily 
complex questions, dividing the form across screens, or 
adding a progress indicator — better usability usually 
equates to lower interaction costs.

When considering the interaction cost in your form design, 
keep in mind that it will vary depending on the user. Each 
form element contributes to the interaction cost in some 
way, but the true impact of each piece will be determined 
by the user.

In general, walking the user around the form while holding 
their hand is less difficult for them. Seeing questions in tiny 
groups rather than a big list of entry fields is both less 
demanding and less daunting.

On the other hand, wasting time on a simple question 
merely because the user isn't sure what the answer should 
be is incredibly inconvenient.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Designing Form
Keep in mind – “The longer your form will be, lower will be 
the conversion rate”. With each successive question, a 
certain percentage of users will give up before reaching the 
finish. Users today don't want to wait and don't want to put 
up effort in filling out forms, so keep your form design 
simple.

Keep only what is absolutely required in your life and let the 
rest go. The length of some forms, such as "Sign in" or 
"Subscribe to a newsletter" forms, isn't an issue because 
they're designed to be brief by default. However, keeping 
the form to two or three entry fields isn't always possible.

That's because, from the user's perspective, losing the effort 
they put in would sabotage the entire experience you had 
prepared. At the very least, the users' hard work will not be 
lost if the internet connection is broken!
Keep things “Short and Simple”
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Breaking it up and offering a progress bar
Again, depending on the situation, creating truly short 
forms may be impossible. Breaking up the shape is a 
practical way to reduce the complexity perception. You 
avoid overwhelming the user at first glance by dividing the 
from across screens.

Users will feel a sense of satisfaction with each screen as an 
added bonus - extra points if you include a progress metre 
with passing screens. Separately, it serves as a reminder of

Ask questions in logical order
In their form design, smart designers attempt to emulate 
true face-to-face discussions – and it would be unusual if, 
upon meeting someone, they asked about your 
grandmother's first time consuming wine. Before going into 
something so personal from your family history, most people 
would expect you to inquire for the person's name, 
occupation, and possibly some background information.

## Sección 48: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.25]

People are easily confounded, thus your form design should 
take advantage of every opportunity to convey the 
message to the user. To give much-needed clarity to any 
form, you can (and should) use the following aspects in form 
design:
Labels: This should be a short, one- or two-word 
description of what the user needs to write/specify. 
Labels should ideally be descriptive and provide a picture 
of the real question.
Placeholders: This should be a short, one- or two-word 
description of what the user needs to write/specify. 
Labels should ideally be descriptive and provide a picture 
of the real question.
Masks: They are especially useful for areas that require a 
specified format, such dates or credit card numbers. They
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

serve as a foundation, allowing users to avoid guessing 
how to convey information.
Validation is a Must
Few things are more irritating than completing a form only 
to receive a confusing error message at the last minute. 
Inline validation is the most effective approach to reassure 
consumers that they answered the question correctly or to 
alert them when anything is incorrect.
Inline validation is a fantastic feature to utilize on your form 
because it inspires users by telling them that everything is

Use the right input controls

because they are the users' way of moving around and

preference, or making a decision.

on what users want or need. Even more intimidating is the 
practice of using pre-checked boxes to create consent for

3. Validation is non-nigotiable
The form UI must include some form of validation, 
regardless of the type of form you're building. You should 
notify users as soon as possible if the data they entered 
cannot be accepted; ideally, you should also inform them of 
the reason why the data is incorrect.

This is because no one enjoys thinking they've completed a 
task only to discover at the last minute that they need to 
return. This unexpected awakening might be aggravating 
and lead to the user throwing in the towel. Instead, reassure 
people that everything is fine – and when something goes 
wrong, explain how to remedy it.

Navigation
The discipline of navigation design entails developing, 
assessing, and implementing methods enabling users to 
navigate across a website or app.

The way users interact with and use your products is heavily 
influenced by navigation. It's the least frustrating route for 
your customer to move from point A to point B, and even 
point C.

Designers use a combination of design patterns, including 
links, labels, and other UI elements, to create these 
engaging encounters. These patterns provide useful 
information and make it easy to interact with items.

When it comes to maximizing the user experience,

menu, a dropdown, or tabs.

Your user may have problems using your product if the

Below are some techniques to improve your navigation 
design, which is a make-or-break part of UI design:
1. Content First
Before sketching a single wireframe, create the content. The 
navigation structure should shape your message to the 
world, not the other way around. Websites that are 
innovative, combine creative concepts with a useful 
purpose. Even the best-designed navigation won't keep you 
on the page if the content isn't good. Create navigation to 
direct the user experience after the content has been 
created.
Navigation supports Content
 The Menu - It is the first place customers look when they 
want to know what's on the site.
 Breadcrumbs - Similar to chapter titles in a book, 
breadcrumbs provide context for a situation. Because 
many users do not want to arrive at the homepage, 
orienting is critical.
 Filters — Filters can help manage information-heavy 
websites. 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

 Links — Assist users in connecting disparate pieces of 
information.
Content Should Dictate Navigation Structure
Great content takes a wide variety of shapes and forms. The 
navigation structure should match the flow of the content. 
“Navigation reveals content” (Steve Krug, Don’t Make Me 
Think). The site’s links, positioning, and labels should work 
together to drive the content to the forefront.
2. Placement
There are several options for navigation: Do you want to 
scroll or click? Should you read or watch? Do you want to 
enter data or go back? We often have to make decisions in 
two-dimensional space without knowing what will happen 
next. A website with good usability gives the user 
organisation and visual cues to help them navigate.

The order in which action options are presented on the page 
has an impact on how users respond. Our brains are 
conditioned to prioritise and filter data. People who speak 
languages written left to right (such as English) pay more 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

attention to the right.

According to Jakob Nielsen's research, individuals read 
pages in an F-Shaped pattern, with a few horizontal scans 
across the top followed by a vertical scan from top to 
bottom.

Because of these behavioural patterns, the most visible 
locations for a menu are (1) horizontally across the top or (2) 
vertically along the left side.
Horizontal Navigation
The majority of websites employ some type of horizontal 
menu. The horizontal navigation bar is easy to scan because 
we read content across the page. It has become a standard 
that users are aware of and anticipate.

What is Animation?
The method of animating UI elements to improve a 
product's interactivity is known as UI animation. Animation 
is used by UX and UI designers to lead users through the 
interface, notify them of changes, affect their decisions, and 
show a relationship between items, among other things. The 
mechanical sense of a website or app is also reduced by UI 
animation, resulting in a far more natural and intuitive 
experience.

Designers used to overburden their interfaces with 
animated trinkets and bright, flashing colours in the early 
days of the digital age. However, as we learned more about 
the psychology of how people engage with digital 
interfaces, it became evident that using too much beautiful 
animation detracted from the site's quality—and even 
caused users to leave. Designers gradually became more 
aware of the functional benefits of animation, transforming

What is Microinteraction?
Micro-interactions are small occurrences that serve a single 
goal and can be found all over your smartphone and within 
apps. Their goal is to delight the user by creating an 
engaging, friendly, and, dare we say, human experience.

Importance of animation in UI design
Users have come to expect powerful, functional, and unique 
interfaces that are easy to navigate as the number of 
websites and apps grows tenfold every day. Humancentered, intuitive, and responsive digital products are 
required in today's market. Animation is one of the ways 
that designers accomplish all of the above (and more).

By recreating the sensation of engaging with a physical 
object in real life, animation adds a key human touch to 
digital interfaces. This makes the user's experience easier 
and more natural, and it minimises cognitive burden. UI

animations serve as guideposts from point A to point B, yet 
they're often so subtle that you'd never see them. Animation 
also relieves stress by giving real-time updates and 
feedback, ensuring that the user is kept up to date at all 
times.

Animation, on the other hand, can make a user interface 
more enjoyable and engaging. It's critical to have a visually 
appealing interface, and just the perfect amount of motion 
can help you stand out from the crowd. Consider your 
digital product to be a stew, and animation to be a spice 
rack. Using only one spice may result in a dull and uninviting 
stew. On the other hand, employing too many different 
spices in your stew can result in an overwhelming and 
perplexing flavour. To make the stew appetising and 
nutritious, you must understand how to add exactly the 
proper quantity of spice.

Different transition types

1. Linear
Objects with a linear animation type move at the same 
speed for the duration of the animation. They will be exactly 
in the middle of their journey for the duration of the 
animation.
Please keep in mind that linear animations lack realism. In 
the actual world, most items must accelerate to reach a 
specific speed. Consider what would happen if an 
automobile started at 100 km/h. It must first accelerate for 
several seconds.

2. Ease In
Objects with the Ease In animation type begin moving (or 
changing state) gently, only to speed once the animation's 
half-time has gone. It's a little like driving a fast automobile.
3. Ease Out
Objects with Ease Out animations start swiftly and end 
slowly, in contrast to objects with Ease In motions. It's similar 
to a braking car that is slowing down after a quick drive.

Follow these tips to improve your UI design 
using illustrations
Adding graphics to your user interface can improve the user 
experience in a variety of ways. You can use them in your 
designs to help users understand a complex concept, 
communicate essential information, or just hold their 
attention.
1. Get the main point across faster than text
Illustrations are frequently used by designers to help users 
comprehend what they need to do. These might be dynamic 
or basic static graphics.

Drawings in lessons and onboarding pages make the 
process more entertaining and understandable. You're 
essentially demonstrating how to do something. Similarly,

Furthermore, it's ideal for skimmers who skip over the text 
and rely solely on images to get what they're looking for 
during surfing sessions.

Finally, illustrations are ideal for working with limited screen 
real estate, such as when developing for mobile interfaces 
or adopting a mobile-first design strategy. Tutorials and 
tooltips, for example.

In tutorials and tooltips, picture’s informative power is clear. 
Illustrations are used in this context to clarify a certain step 
or to inspire the user to take action. Adding graphics to user 
interfaces improves the user experience in this way.

2. Engage the user through their journey
A user's goal when interacting with a website or user 
interface is to complete a certain task. Their user journey is 
made up of the steps they take to complete that task. The 
usage of UI graphics can help designers create interfaces 
that improve the user journey (and thus the user 
experience).

You can use graphics to interest the user and make the 
procedure easier in this instance. This is very helpful for 
creating long or complicated onboarding processes.

Cards
Salesforce, Apple, Netflix, Pinterest, Twitter, Instagram, and 
the BBC are some of the most well-known companies in the 
world. There is one element that all of these businesses have 
in common that you may not have noticed. They all use a 
card-based user interface.

Cards, cards, cards - they're all over the place. If you pay 
attention, you'll notice that most apps and platforms these 
days use UI cards in some form or another.

But why are UI cards so popular, and which apps and 
platforms stand to gain the most from them?

Here in this chapter, you'll learn everything about card from 
the definition to how you can design the best card by 
yourself. So let's start the chapter by understanding what UI 
card actually is.
What is a UI card?
Cards are UI elements that serve as content containers. 
Cards often have an image, title, description, call to action, 
and subheadings or icons. The concept that each card 
symbolises one idea, item, or piece of content binds the 
cards together. A card is a user interface design pattern 
that bundles similar information in a flexible-size container 
that visually resembles a playing card, according to Nielsen 
Norman Group.

The modular design method, which focuses on independent 
transforming pieces, includes card-based design. In UI 
design, cards are self-contained components that can be 
rearranged or altered. It is now one of the most popular 
design layouts. There are a couple of strong reasons for this.
Advantages of card UI design
Users' attention is drawn to UI cards by the colourful picture 
element, little bits of information, easy-to-click CTAs, and 
other elements. They have a higher scroll rate and have a 
greater impact than lists.
Highly responsive
Easy-to-understand 
The fundamental benefit of UI cards is that they are 
extremely adaptable. They're transformative because of 
their rectangular shape. As a result, UI cards appear great 
on any screen size.
UI cards are simple and straightforward design elements. 
They reveal small amounts of information that are easy to
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

assimilate.
UX-friendly and intuitive
Clickable
Attractive and modern-looking 
The card interface style is familiar to most users, and they 
can easily move through it. The user experience is incredibly 
intuitive thanks to the rectangular design and basic 
clickable elements.
The UI card is an excellent tool for getting customers to 
interact with your product. Users want to click and share 
right away when they see compact and well-designed cards 
with the proper CTA.
Even though card-based design has been around for a 
decade, it is still a current design approach. This layout's 
core visual element makes it simple to make any product 
appealing.
Is Card always the best solution?
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

UI cards are fantastic. They are adored by designers, 
developers, and users alike. Is card UI design, on the other 
hand, truly a one-size-fits-all solution? This is the point at 
which you must make a decision. Cards, in the hands of 
talented designers, can transform practically on any web or 
mobile app. However, there are a few factors to keep in 
mind:@
/ List layouts are more hierarchical than UI cards. They're 
more useful for browsing than searching. If you're 
working with data or elements where order is critical, a 
more vertically structured list layout is preferable.@
/ Another compelling reason to reconsider using UI cards 
in your website or application is that they are widely 
utilized nowadays. As a result, standing out in a crowd is 
difficult. Card design, on the other hand, is never dull 
when the proper visuals are used.@
/ Because cards take up more space, the list is more 
efficient for scanning titles quickly. The same principle 
applies to content that is repeated. So, if you need your
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

$ user to wade through a lot of data or components where 
titles are more important than graphics, you should think 
about using a different layout.,
$ Cards are versatile components in and of themselves, 
but only to a limited extent. The amount of rectangular 
pieces is always the deciding factor in the end. So, if you 
want to represent your goods in a more grandiose way, 
you should think about different design options.

about integrating cards in your app or platform. We 
gathered some samples of well-designed user interface

 Less is more in this case. Do not overcrowd your UI cards

Points to take away
It's no surprise that UI cards are one of the most popular 
design elements. UI cards are adaptable and responsive, 
user-friendly, and simple to navigate and share. Cards 
conserve your users' attention and allow them to focus on 
the content, while also providing a clean and easy-tounderstand user interface.

Almost any type of business can benefit from this design. It 
is not, however, a universal solution. If your users will be 
working with data where order and search are vital, use a 
list or grid style, as UI cards are better for exploring 
material. In many circumstances, however, cards might be a 
comprehensive solution for a web or mobile application, or 
they can reflect a single aspect of it. 

Keep your UI cards simple, but never boring, to create a 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

successful UI card layout. Make the entire card clickable, 
and use effects, motion, colors, shading, and space to 
entice people to engage with it.

Screens
As of 2022, there are almost 22 billion devices connected to 
the internet with almost 7 billion smartphone users.

Every user expects that every website or application is

What are Gradients?
The gradual transition from one hue to another is known as 
a gradient. It allows the designer to essentially make a 
brand-new hue.

It gives designs a new dimension and gives the thing more 
reality, which helps the product stand out. Gradients 
essentially add depth.

A gradient is a progressive transition from one colour to 
another (or, if you're feeling particularly creative, from one 
colour to another colour to another colour; gradients aren't 
restricted to two shades). Gradients are also known as 
colour transitions.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Gradients can combine or change between colours that are 
similar (such as various blue hues or a mild orange to a dark 
red), as well as colours that are entirely unrelated or in 
opposition (like purple and red or blue and yellow).
The gradient fashion is very adaptable. It might be overt or 
understated, the centrepiece of a design or a supporting 
component. 

Gradients may also provide new colour combinations that 
feel fresh and contemporary by mixing and blending various 
colour tones, giving designs a really one-of-a-kind vibe.
The gradient design adds depth and dimension to 
the otherwise flat fox graphic.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

The possibilities are unlimited! You may utilize gradients to 
add depth to a flat design, provide an intriguing texture for 
a backdrop, or give a photo new life (and color!)
Why are gradients currently so popular?
Why then is the gradient trend so popular right now? 
because they are so captivating and eye-catching. These 
strikingly brilliant color transitions stand out for their 
intensity and serve to boost any design.
Why then is the gradient trend so popular right now? 
because they are so captivating and eye-catching. These

too retro. Was nostalgia enough to have individuals

In addition, individuals are obsessing over the gradient

Because there is so much material that consumers are

Types of Gradients

Linear
Radial

Reflected gradients are made by creating a conventional

Gradients may have a far deeper purpose in the UI design

discussed. Here are a few reasons why gradients should be 
used more commonly in interface designs.
Place emphasis on certain elements
In addition to being visually appealing, gradients may also 
be quite useful. Particularly with UI gradients, the change in 
color may be utilized to direct users' attention from one area

postulates that the eye flows from bright to dark naturally,

Easily elevate simple concept

personality.
Adding with light and shadows
Shadows and highlights are all types of UI gradients, 
however novice designers might not be aware of this. The 
truth is that every color may have depth by properly 
manipulating light and shadow; there is no such thing as a 
completely "flat" color.

How to use gradients to your advantage?
You may apply gradient in a variety of ways to improve your 
design. You are aware of this, but do you know how? You are 
aware of gradients and their uses now, but in order for the 
gradient to function for you, there are a few things you need 
to understand. So let me to answer to your query: "How can 
you make it work for you?"
1. Choose the right colors
The colors you select are the gradient trend's most crucial 
component. Choose complementary colours (colours that 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

are opposite each other on the colour wheel), analogous 
colours (three colours next to each other on the colour 
wheel), triadic colours, or colours that "work" together 
according to colour theory for the gradient trend to look 
well-designed. For example, a gradient that fades from light 
blue to dark blue (three colours spaced equally apart on the 
colour wheel).

Additionally, it's crucial to select hues that correspond to 
the emotions and feelings you want to arouse in your 
audience. Do you want visitors to your website to experience 
peace and tranquilly? It will work if there is a gradient that 
changes from a gentle green to blue. Do you want 
consumers to be prepared to buy when they are? Choose 
more striking hues that can inspire consumers to purchase, 
such as red or orange. Make careful to keep colour 
psychology in mind when selecting colours for your gradient 
because it is a strong tool.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Last but not least, make sure the colors you select for your 
gradients work with your current brand colors. For example, 
if your business's color palette is red and blue, creating a 
gradient with lime green and bright yellow is definitely not 
going to look great.
2. Be careful of color choices
Unsurprisingly, developing UI gradients adheres to one of 
the most important design principles. An essential talent for 
every designer is the ability to use colour to produce certain 
effects. Color theory is a fundamental concept for designers 
that permeates all facets of the profession.

Examining websites for vehicles targeted for various

straightforward brilliance of their automobiles. 

In contrast, the Honda Civic website, which targets 
millennials and young professionals globally, displays the

vibrant colours in the user interface (UI), often yellows and 
blues, to represent the brand's youthful and joyful side.

Use complimentary colours as well; while gradients in user 
interfaces are a great way to liven up a design, when done

3. Use gradients wisely
Gradients are useful, as we already stated. They may be 
used as an overlay, to provide depth to a flat design, to add 
visual interest to a backdrop of a single hue, etc. The list is 
endless.

But just because something is adaptable doesn't imply that 
you should take it as your new design mantra. Less is 
(possibly) more when it comes to gradients, just like any 
other trend. Don't complicate the design. Don't go too far. 
Gradients are important, but make sure you use them 
properly.
4. Spend time on smoothing transitions
It will take time and work to master a gradient, and it's 
doubtful that you'll succeed in creating one that is 
aesthetically arresting on your first try. Play around with 
color stops, try adding or removing gradient stages, or 
change the gradient's color palette. 

Also bear in mind that long transitions aren't always the 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

best option; in some situations, such as when shading tiny 
parts, quick transitions can emphasize motion more 
effectively. Try out three-stepped gradients; these may be 
effective when utilizing subdued tones. Like with anything 
else in UI design, some tinkering and fiddling is necessary, 
but we promise the end result will be worthwhile.
5. Know your audience
5. Make it fun
You must thoroughly understand your audience and the 
types of designs they will respond to if you want your 
gradients to have the desired effect. A vibrant pink and 
yellow gradient, for instance, is perhaps not the ideal choice 
if you're selling to a group of more established 
entrepreneurs.

Knowing your audience will help you select the ideal 
gradient, including the colors to use, how to incorporate 
them into your design, and whether to use a loud or subtle 
style.
Have fun with this trend; it's enjoyable! To determine what 
Pixsel Academy

gradients both as the focus point and in the background, 
and have fun with overlays. The idea is that this isn't 
anything that should be taken too seriously. Therefore, have 
fun and loosen up with your gradients!
Conclusion
In conclusion, don't be scared of gradients! When it comes 
to creating extensive and appealing designs, a gradient 
might be another tool in your UI designer's quiver. 
Additionally, it may give designs that might otherwise lack 
"that particular something" a new depth. 

When the industry moves away from the "flat UI" tendency, 
shading and highlights will be helpful in giving designs more 
dimension. Try several things and see what works; you'll 
undoubtedly find some use for UI designs.

The gradient trend appears to be here to stay, unlike other

The distance a shadow extends (or contracts) from the edge 
of the object it is applied to is known as its spread. From the 
element, a spread with a plus number (1 to infinity) expands 
outward. A spread that is negative (from -1 to infinity) pulls 
an element's edge spread inward.
The blur of a shadow indicates how much the shadow's 
edge is... blurred. You will get a sharp edge with a blur of 0, a 
blur of 10 will blur 10 pixels in and out from the shadow's 
edge, and so on. The shadow will get larger and more 
diffuse the higher the number.

Neumorphism
A novel method of skeuomorphic building is known as 
"neumorphism." Neumorphism gives the entire design style 
a fresh focus even if it is connected to Skeuomorphism. 
Here, the focus is on the color scheme rather than on how 
the real and virtual worlds compare or resemble one 
another.

You read that accurately; that is true. The main goal of 
neomorphism is to entirely alter the hue of the user's 
experience on the screen.
Neumorphism Design
The fundamental tenet of neumorphism is that the 
background color must match the color of the elements. 
Additionally, to give the impression that the parts are 
extensions of the surroundings, the highlighting is done in a 
subtle yet effective manner. Each UI feature is projected 
with a light shadow on the upper left corner and a dark 
shadow on the bottom right. 

As a result, it appears as though the screen's light source is 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

in the upper-left corner, casting a shadow on the lower right. 
Instead of appearing to float, the pieces seem to emerge 
from the background.

There are other strong reasons why Neumorphism stands 
out differently in today's UI in addition to the elements.

The fundamental ideas required for Neumorphism to 
function are the stacking of shadows and negative X and Y 
values shown below in the image example.
To indicate interactivity

shadow should get smaller to let the user know they've 
engaged with that particular element. This is also true in 
reality; when a button is pressed, the object's height falls 
and a smaller shadow is created as a result.
To create visual depth
You will need a technique to stack components on top of 
one another while guaranteeing that they remain visually 
distinct from one another unless you are designing with a 
flat interface style. By enabling you to layer components 
using the z-axis, shadows can aid with this. Using this 
method, you can open dropdown elements or display 
modals that appear "above" the page's content while still 
distinguishing the list from other inputs.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

To create a visual hierarchy
To show a hierarchy of elements, visual depth can also be 
used. What do you want a user to focus on initially? The user 
will be forced to focus on the objects in the order they 
appear to them by stacking that item on top of other design 
components and using shadows to show each separate 
layer. 

In mobile applications, where a menu might appear above 
other elements, this is a more often utilized technique. By 
including a shadow, you can push that piece to the front of 
your users' attention. This is crucial if you want your user to 
engage with aspects like cookie or privacy regulations that 
you are trying to communicate.

Blurs
Blurs were briefly discussed in the elements of a shadow 
section, but now we'll go into more detail about what they 
are and how to apply them. In contrast to shadows, which 
must be applied from an element's edge, blurs can be 
applied directly to an element.
Types of Blur
Depending on whether you're working on a graphic or an 
interface, there are many blur kinds you might employ. We'll 
go over the ones you can apply to interface design, but if 
you're interested in learning more about blurs, check out the 
gaussian, motion, and radial blur types online!
Background
Only the element's background receives a background blur 
rather than to the actual element. Imagine you have a 
square with a picture as the background. The image would 
appear blurred if you applied a background blur, but the 
edge of the element would still be razor-sharp.

## Sección 68: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.454]

Layer
Layer blur, on the other hand, blurs the entire element. 
Consequently, not only are the edges of the element 
blurred, but also the background. If you want a background 
element to disappear so you may draw visitors' attention to 
something else, you might use this technique. Or you might 
include it into a design as a decorative element.
To focus attention
Blurs have a lot of uses but the most common use in 
interface design is to focus the users’ attention. You will 
likely have seen it used when modals are opened or when a 
Using Blurs in UI design
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

navigation element is opened on mobile, you can blur the 
existing background elements to bring the user’s attention 
to the item they have just opened. 

If you use an iPhone with an up-to-date operating system 
you will see this in effect every time you use the control 
center and open a specific item. The item you have opened 
is in focus but the background gets blurred.
To increase the legibility of overlaid text
If you are using text over images or video you have two 
options, a hard background or a blurred background. A hard 
background can be good for subtitles but a blurred
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

background allows you to keep a visually pleasing aesthetic 
without compromising on accessibility. You will want to 
make sure the images you are using behind the blur all 
follow a similar color pallet though, as depending on the 
text you are using, it may not be visible on a blurred dark or 
light background.
As a decorative effect
Blurs can also be very aesthetically pleasing to look at, and 
although interface design is about usability, it’s also about 
making sure the things we create look good. Blurs can be 
used on background images to bring focus to graphic 
elements laid on top. This is common in the ‘hero’ section of 
websites when companies want to use imagery but don’t 
want it to steal focus. It can also be used to great effect in 
animations and abstract elements.

Soft Skills

The creation of a digital product (apps, websites, etc.) is 
both challenging and rewarding. Because no two design 
jobs are same, the issues that arise are also unique. While 
anyone can learn to design by taking classes, through 
Ebooks, or following YouTube tutorials, being a great and 
successful UX or UI designer is difficult. Designers must 
develop not just hard talents but also soft skills in order to 
stand out. Soft skills, if nothing else, are essential for landing 
a job as a UX/UI Designer.

According to Icons8, 92 percent of decision-makers believe 
that soft skills are as important as or more important than 
technical skills when recruiting UX UI designers. 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

While putting hard skills on a resume is easy, Soft skills, on 
the other hand, are difficult to quantify and often relate to 
emotional intelligence, personality qualities, and situational 
awareness. These non-technical abilities can make the 
difference between a good and a great UX designer!

A UX/UI designer's skill set extends beyond hard talents. 
While technical skills are vital, UI experts must also master 
personality (soft) skills in order to succeed in a competitive 
industry. These abilities appeal to one's emotional 
intelligence and problem-solving abilities, which ultimately 
makes all the difference. Take a look at some of the most 
important soft skills for designers.
Soft Skills for UI designers
Now let’s learn about the soft skills required to be a perfect 
UI designer.
1. Effective and clear Communication 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

So, you've come up with a fantastic project design idea? 
Great! Can you, however, communicate your thoughts and 
explain the reasoning behind the design? You won't be able 
to nail the presentation if you don't have solid 
communication abilities, which is vital when working with 
other designers or team members.

UX UI designers need effective communication skills to 
express their ideas to team members and clients. And, in 
order to accomplish any job successfully, communication is 
essential. Because no one knows your thoughts better than 
you, it's critical that you can boldly express what you're 
thinking.
2. Empathy 
If the designer does not grasp the client's wishes and needs, 
it is impossible to build a product that meets those goals. 
Simply put, you can't grasp what someone else desires or is 
going through unless you put yourself in their shoes.

This is what empathy entails. It's about being able to 
comprehend other people's emotions, sentiments, goals,
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

and thoughts. An empathic designer may readily 
incorporate those insights or understandings into the design 
project, resulting in experiences that are not only relevant, 
but also useful to those who will use them.

Talking to end-users via user interviews, usability testing, 
and surveys is one of the greatest ways to learn their needs. 
Another excellent strategy is observation.
3. Creativity 
Technical talents, to be honest, can be taught. Creativity, on 
the other hand, emerges from within and is defined as the 
act of transforming creative, one-of-a-kind thoughts into 
reality. One of the most sought-after soft skills for designers 
is the ability to go outside the box and create something 
unique.

Designers with a creative bent of mind are capable of 
recognising or producing brand-new, distinctive ideas, as 
well as perceiving everyday objects in novel ways. 
Creativity, on the other hand, is a skill that can be refined 
and sharpened by keeping up with current trends and being 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

inspired by the work of other designers.
4. Problem-solving skills 
Design is about resolving issues and making things run more 
smoothly and easily for end users. After a sequence of tries 
and errors, a designer's task is to choose the optimal 
solution. After comprehending the precise brief supplied by 
the client and working on the constructive comments 
provided on the design prototype, the difficulties must be 
addressed from all perspectives.

Problems are bound to arise no matter what project you're 
working on, especially when you least expect them. Great 
problem-solving abilities aid in remaining calm and 
overcoming obstacles.
5. Open-minded and Flexile 
Open-mindedness isn't the first ability that comes to mind 
when thinking about UX design, but it's crucial. It would be 
impossible to come up with answers or excellent designs if 
you were narrow-minded. Designers must be open to new 
ideas and embrace the opinions of others. In addition, one 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

must be able to quickly adapt to new design trends and 
integrate them. Clients frequently add new requirements or 
decide to change everything, so being adaptable and 
open-minded is critical to overcoming such obstacles.
6. Passionate 
Being passionate is certainly a must-have soft talent in any 
sector, but it's especially true for a UX designer because a 
lack of interest will immediately reflect in the design of a 
product. If your designers aren't enthusiastic about what 
they do, they're unlikely to succeed in the field of UX design.

A designer's career entails difficult workloads, strange 
hours, and a barrage of criticism from clients, bosses, and 
coworkers. Professional difficulties can swiftly tyre one 
down unless one has the ambition, enthusiasm, and desire 
to learn.
7. Love for constructive criticism! 
There is no such thing as an ideal design. Because there are 
no defined algorithms or mathematics, and people's goals
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

and requirements are constantly changing, a flawless 
design is a fiction. As a result, a UX/UI designer must 
recognise this and be willing to accept constructive 
criticism. Designers must work well with others and be willing 
to adapt their designs in response to input.
Points to be noted 
Designers' soft talents are just as vital as their technical 
skills, if not more so. A UI designer who possesses a mix of 
hard, soft, as well as questioning abilities can design a 
brilliant digital product.

Exercise 1: Analyze your gut instinct to strengthen it
Successful UI designers, like any other open-ended creative 
effort, have a strong "gut instinct" for what will look 
beautiful. One of the most common concerns I hear from

websites to visit for inspiration, layouts to use, and so on. 
Some of these may be things I've never done before, but I 
have a feeling they'll work — whether because I did 
something similar on a project a year ago, or because I saw 
something similar on an app I used only last week.

This way of describing gut instinct makes it appear more 
approachable. It's understanding what options are

Exercise 2: Copywork
Even though copywork is an excellent way to improve your 
design skills, few designers really practise it.

In a nutshell, copywork is simply replicating an amazing 
existing design in your preferred design tool pixel-for-pixel.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

Copywork isn't a novel UI design exercise. From painters to 
writers, everyone has mimicked the greats before them for 
practise:'
> To adapt his forefather's world-class cadence and 
phrasing, Jack London plagiarised large sections of 
Rudyard Kipling's writing.'
> Robert Louis Stevenson would study passages of text 
that he thought were particularly lovely, then recite them 
word for word from memory.'
> Ben Franklin used a form of copywork in which he took 
notes on each sentence in an essay and then attempted 
to duplicate the essay by reading his notes and 
comparing the outcomes a few days later.'
> "The artist ought first to exercise his hand by reproducing 
drawings from the hand of a good master," Leonardo da 
Vinci recommended this as the first painting exercise for 
any of his students.

Copywork has fallen out of favour, despite the fact that it 
was once a foundational practise for some of the world's 
finest artists. Nowadays, it's seen as routine, unimaginative,
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

and plagiarised.

I expect to see a reversal of this tendency among UI 
designers, as copywork is a great way to broaden your 
"design vocabulary."

The basic idea is that when you reproduce a design pixel for 
pixel, you're compelled to redo every decision made by the 
original designer. Which typeface should I use? How big is 
it? What's the layout like? Which graphics, backgrounds, 
and decorations are you using? You become engrossed in 
the minute design decisions made by talented designers. As 
you re-create those judgments, you'll realise that the 
original made choices that you wouldn't have taken, and 
used tactics or tricks that you wouldn't have picked up just 
by looking at the design.

As a "shadow," use a paint stroke
Letter space is generous in uppercase type
The bottom of the seal, the header, and the pineapple all 
have a watercolour smudge. It's where a shadow might be,

Without a doubt, uppercase text provides a strong sense of

almost all of it has changed letter spacing.

Before opting to duplicate Dann Petty's design, I had seen it.

what they do well or what you lack. Copy something with

Exercise 4: Style Tiles
If you're anything like me, you can't just collect your 
favourite fonts and not want to utilise them. And you can't 
look at all these gorgeous websites without wanting to try 
out some of the same concepts.

This is when the use of style tiles comes into play.
Style tiles are little artboards that include all of the brand 
aspects you're considering, such as font, colour, form 
controls, and a logo. They don't show you the final product, 
but they do give you an idea of what it will look like.

They're brief, one-off explorations of a particular UI concept

you could have.

and debatable. My client and I don't have to talk about the 
brand in such broad terms from now on; we can simply 
advise what's working and what's not from a notion like this.

But first, let's pause for a moment.

Despite the fact that style tiles are an excellent first UI 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

deliverable for a client's website, I recommend them as a 
personal exercise for honing your UI skills. Why?

They're low-risk, quick to put together, and simple to try out.

When you come across a typeface and think to yourself, 
"This would be perfect for a premium brand!" test it out! 
Download that font (or a nice free replacement if it's too 
pricey), pick some classy photographs on Unsplash, choose 
a colour palette (OK, OK, simply use grayscale + gold), and 
play around.

By the way, the "experiment" portion is crucial. The sooner 
you can cycle between (A) generating an idea, (B) putting it 
into action, and (C) assessing it, the faster you will learn. 
There's nothing stopping this process from taking seconds 
with style tiles (at least as an individual exercise). There was 
no need for approval, there were no UX limits, there was no 
budget, and there was no bureaucracy.

You alone, working on your trade one step at a time.

Exercise 5: Personal Project Mockups
Working on personal design projects is the fifth and last UI 
activity I propose.
This translates to: 
j You are not compensated for them (at least not by a 
client or employer) 
j You – and only you – have complete creative control 
over the project.

These could take any of the following forms:%
 You create an app that you wish existed but doesn't.%
 You remodel an existing app or website that you believe 
may be bettered.%
 You create a website for a non-profit or other charitable 
organisation, and you may send it to them at the end.

(I'm referring to the part where "you alone" have a complete 
creative authority.) Maybe you're working on a side project 
with a partner. If your partner has a lot of feedback on the 
design, you have two tasks: (1) understand your partner's 
feedback on the design, and (2) strive toward something 
that both of you are happy with. This is much more difficult – 
especially for a beginner designer – than simply trying to 
create something you enjoy. So I strongly advise you to look 
for a project where the criterion for success is whether or not 
you believe it looks beautiful. Is that how it works in the real 
world? No, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no However, this 
is a learning exercise in which we eliminate real-world 
limitations to learn more efficiently.)

## Sección 74: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.34]

Choosing a style tile that you particularly enjoyed working 
on and turning it into a more full-fledged app or website is a 
simple method to choose a personal project. There are three 
possible outcomes here, each of which will influence how 
much of the design you complete before reaching the point 
of diminishing returns:

1. A project for self-study - This is something you develop 
just for your own educational benefit; you don't necessarily 
intend to show it to anyone (though you might show it to 
more experienced designers for comments). For a project 
like this, you may skip a lot of the UI phase or ignore some 
real-world business limitations. Fine. This is primarily about 
improving your visual talents, such as color, font, and so on. 
Focus on the "primary screens" of the app for projects like 
these to get the most bang for your money — the homepage, 
profiles, dashboards, feeds, and articles – where the user 
will spend the most of their time.

2. A portfolio project - For a project you’re particularly 
proud of and design out in its entirety, you might consider 
putting it on your portfolio. This is getting a bit beyond the
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

scope of this article, but you’ll basically want to design out 
the entire app – not only the main screens, but also auxiliary 
experiences such as error states, loading pages, first-timeusing-the-app flows, etc. I’ll be writing more on creating a 
solid portfolio on Design Hacks.

3. A real app or site - If you or a partner have the 
development skills, what started as a quick project might 
become a real app. This is really beyond the scope of this 
article, but as you work on these 5 exercises over time, you 
will start to find yourself closer and closer to completing a 
real app. At that point, it might be time to graduate to a real 
curriculum.

Creating some realistically branded screens for personal 
project apps, on the other hand, will tie everything you've 
done so far together – the design details you've noticed and 
recorded, the lessons you've learned from copying the pros, 
the fonts you've collected, and the styles you've put 
together.

So, first and foremost, allow me to share something with you. 
Every brain functions in a unique way. That is a proven fact. 
We cannot compare ourselves to one another or mindlessly

But… Creativity is a big power and the most crucial aspect

This is my own way, thus the steps I'm mentioning are mine. 
I'm not the best user interface designer, and I don't claim to 
be on either. But… I do know how to approach UI design. 
And let me tell you exactly how you can too…

The product development process includes UI/UX design. It 
determines whether or not your product will succeed in the 
future.

The most effective technique to create a good UI design is 
to combine structure, content, and user experience in a 
specific way that allows your company to achieve its 
objectives.

When it comes to UI Design, take a user-centered approach. 
Adopting the method allows you to create micro-moments 
that are straightforward, intuitive, and engaging for your 
users. As a result, you'll stand out from the throng! If you
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

want to be an excellent UI UX designer, you must first 
master this skill.
Follow my steps
The UI/UX Design Process is a methodology for polishing 
your user interfaces to make them the best they can be for 
your business if you follow it. If this procedure is not 
followed, we may find ourselves having to remodel ourselves 
on a regular basis!

So, let's get started on the subject!

According to my pattern, The entire UI/UX design process 
may be broken down into five distinct stages. Every single 
stage will be scrutinised by the responsible department of 
your company, and as a result, it will be nearly perfect!
Product
Definition
Research
Analysis
Design
Validation
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

1. Product Definition
The initial step in the user design process is product 
definition. The team in charge of gathering user needs will 
do so based on their business context.

It is critical since it is during this phase that the true extent 
of the product and its existence is realised.

It's simple: inform your UI/UX designers about the 
requirements before beginning work!

The Design Team, Business Manager, and Product Manager 
are all active in this phase. Clients should be consulted by 
the entire team in their surroundings. Examine their 
requirements within the context of your business.

User Personas, User Stories, and Use Case Diagrams are 
important deliverables of this phase.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

2. Research
For a designer, the most important aspect is research. For 
the current customer proposal, the design team investigates 
how the current system functions. The following are the 
three primary functions at this stage:%
* Know what the competition is all about.%
* Examining your current domain thoroughly.%
* Examining competitor strategy to see what happens.

Understanding the newest UI/UX trends, design principles, 
and guidelines should also be part of the research process.
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

## Sección 75: Core Concepts & Foundations [Entropy: 0.265]

3. Analysis
Make advantage of the information gathered during the 
research phase in this phase. Create hypothetical personas 
and experience maps using the information you've 
gathered.9
B Hypothetical Personas : Creating hypothetical scenarios 
allows designers to learn more about the many types of 
people who will use your product. It allows for an 
accurate portrayal of the final product to be depicted. 
After delivery, the design team can work out how it will 
look.9
B User Experience Maps : A user experience map depicts 
the flow of users through your final product. All of this is 
accomplished through the use of visual representations 
and correct interactions with clients during the product 
definition phase.
4. Design
We finish up bringing life to the concepts we collected in the 
previous three processes during the design process. Now is 
Pixsel Academy
UI Pedia - A Complete UI Guide

the time to start working on the final graphics. In this step, 
the design team will put the final design into action.

The following are key outcomes of the design phase:

Sketching : Sketching is the first step in the design process. 
Designers frequently create hand-drawn sketches to 
visualise concepts in simple ways. After the sketching 
process, the UX/UI designers can stick to a specific option.
Creating wireframes : A wireframe is a visual representation 
of the page hierarchy and product aspects. A wireframe is 
regarded as the product's backbone. It's also known as the 
design's skeleton. It's largely about the end product's overall 
appearance.

Making Prototypes : Prototypes focus on the look and feel of 
the UI/UX product being designed. It's all about the 
experience of interacting with others. The effect of a 
prototype is similar to that of a simulator.

Creating Design Specifications : User flow and task flow 
diagrams are included in the design specification. It 
describes the UI/UX product's overall working and stylistic 
requirements. It explains how to create excellent user 
experiences by describing the procedures and graphical 
aspects involved.
